simply to quiet his financial fears, he brought it up to his primary care provider during a routine visit and was referred for psychiatric evaluation.\n- Chief Complaint: “I feel like I’m constantly worrying about money and my future, from the moment I wake up until I go to bed, and it’s taking over everything.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S027 (Sleep_Disturbance): Most nights I lie there staring at the ceiling for an hour or two, running numbers in my head about bills and retirement, and even when I finally do drift off I wake up at three or four in the morning with my mind already back on whether I can keep the house and then I can’t get back to sleep. \n- S056 (Excessive_Anxiety_Worry): It feels like there’s a low-grade alarm going off in my head all day, jumping from ‘what if enrollment is low when I open the preschool’ to ‘what if the car dies’ to ‘what if I get sick and can’t work,’ and even little things like buying new crayons for my class turn into a debate about whether I’m going to end up bankrupt in ten years. \n- S057 (Uncontrollable_Worry): Once the “what if” thoughts start—like when I’m checking my bank account or reading an article about the economy—I can’t seem to shut them down, and I’ll catch myself still spinning out scenarios half an hour later even while I’m supposed to be reading a story to the kids or talking with a parent. \n- S059 (Concentration_Difficulty): Lately I’ll be in the middle of writing a lesson plan or talking at a staff meeting and my mind just blanks, like someone switched the channel, and all I can think about is whether I miscalculated the mortgage payoff or forgot to pay a bill. \n- S061 (Muscle_Tension): By the end of most days my shoulders and neck feel like I’ve been hunched under a backpack full of rocks, and I’ll realize I’ve been clenching my jaw and gripping the steering wheel so hard on the drive home that my hands and face ache. \n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: Over the past year I’ve started turning down chances to help lead new projects at school and keep stalling on concrete steps for the preschool because my head feels too cluttered to make decisions, and even at home I let bills and basic chores pile up because I’m so tied in knots worrying about them that I can’t bring myself to deal with them.\n- duration: This level of constant, all-day worry has been my baseline for a little over a year now, ever since about a year after the divorce settlement when I really started planning the preschool and looking hard at my finances.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Or is the anxiety more of a steady, ongoing worry throughout the day?\n\nPatient: It is definitely more of a steady, ongoing worry throughout the day rather than sudden spikes of panic. It feels like a low-grade alarm is constantly running in the background from the moment I wake up until I go to bed. I don't really experience those sudden physical surges or panic attacks, just this non-stop worrying.\n\nDoctor: Have you been using any substances or medicines that can make anxiety worse, like a lot of caffeine, stimulants, decongestants, cannabis, or thyroid medication? And do you have any medical problems such as thyroid issues that started around the same time as the anxiety?\n\nPatient: No, I don't use any of those substances or medications, and I don't drink excessive caffeine or use cannabis. I also don't have any medical issues, like thyroid problems, that started around the same time as my anxiety.\n\nDoctor: Along with the anxiety, have you been feeling depressed most days or lost interest in things you usually enjoy? If yes, has that lasted at least two weeks in a row?"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========

For example, I find myself sitting in my garden unable to enjoy the plants I've tended, or repeatedly checking my bank account instead of practicing my guitar. This has been going on for much longer than two weeks—it has really been my baseline for over a year now.

It first started escalating after my divorce settlement two years ago left me with tighter finances, but it really took off about a year ago when I started planning a preschool venture and looking hard at my financial future.\n\nDoctor: When your worry is at its worst, does it still feel tied mainly to the divorce and financial stress, or has it spread into lots of other areas even when nothing new is happening? Has it continued even during times when those stressors were less intense?\n\nPatient: It has definitely spread to almost everything, and it feels like a constant low-grade alarm going off in my head all day. Even minor things, like buying new crayons for my classroom, now trigger intense anxiety about whether I’m going to end up bankrupt in ten years. It just doesn't let up, even when the bigger stressors are relatively quiet.\n\nDoctor: Along with the constant worry, have you also been having symptoms like feeling restless or on edge, getting tired easily, trouble concentrating, muscle tension, irritability, or problems sleeping? If so, which ones have been happening most days?\n\nPatient: Yes, I’ve been struggling with several of those almost daily. I lie awake for hours running numbers in my head, only to wake up at three in the morning unable to get back to sleep. On top of that, my mind constantly blanks during staff meetings or when I'm writing lesson plans, and my shoulders and neck feel like I'm carrying a heavy backpack of rocks because I'm clenching my jaw so tight.\n\nDoctor: How much is this anxiety interfering with your daily life, like your work, relationships, or taking care of things at home? Have you started avoiding situations or making changes because of the worry?\n\nPatient: It’s interfering a great deal; I’ve started turning down leadership opportunities at school and stalling on my preschool venture because my head is just too cluttered to make decisions. At home, I even let bills and basic chores pile up because I’m so tied in knots with worry that I just can't bring myself to deal with them.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever get sudden surges of intense fear or physical symptoms that peak within minutes, like your heart racing, shaking, sweating, shortness of breath, or feeling like something terrible is about to happen? Or is the anxiety more of a steady, ongoing worry throughout the day?\n\nPatient: It is definitely more of a steady, ongoing worry throughout the day rather than sudden spikes of panic. It feels like a low-grade alarm is constantly running in the background from the moment I wake up until I go to bed. I don't really experience those sudden physical surges or panic attacks, just this non-stop worrying.\n\nDoctor: Have you been using any substances or medicines that can make anxiety worse, like a lot of caffeine, stimulants, decongestants, cannabis, or thyroid medication? And do you have any medical problems such as thyroid issues that started around the same time as the anxiety?\n\nPatient: No, I don't use any of those substances or medications, and I don't drink excessive caffeine or use cannabis. I also don't have any medical issues, like thyroid problems, that started around the same time as my anxiety.\n\nDoctor: Along with the anxiety, have you been feeling depressed most days or lost interest in things you usually enjoy? If yes, has that lasted at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I feel deeply depressed, but I have definitely lost interest and pleasure in the things I used to enjoy. For example, I find myself sitting in my garden unable to enjoy the plants I've tended, or repeatedly checking my bank account instead of practicing my guitar. This has been going on for much longer than two weeks—it has really been my baseline for over a year now.\n\nDoctor: During this past year, have you also felt down, sad, or hopeless most days for at least two weeks at a time, or is it mainly anxiety and loss of enjoyment without feeling depressed? Did those low-mood feelings begin only after the divorce and financial stress, or do they persist even when those stressors are quieter?\n\nPatient: It is definitely more about the constant anxiety and losing touch with my hobbies, rather than feeling down, sad, or hopeless in a depressed way. My mind is just constantly racing with financial calculations, and I feel completely consumed, distracted, and exhausted by the non-stop worry about my future."
  }

Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
